1. Background 
 
The Arizona Department of Health Services (ADHS) Bureau of Nutrition and Physical Activity (BNPA) administers 
funds provided by the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA) for the operation of the Special 
Supplemental Nutrition Program for Women, Infants and Children (WIC) and Breastfeeding Peer Counseling 
Program (BFPC) for the State of Arizona. The USDA nutrition programs are discretionary, and each provides a 
specific service to women, infants, and children who are low income and at nutritional ri sk. The overall goal of all 
the USDA Nutrition Programs is to increase food security and reduce hunger by providing eligible participants 
access to nourishing food and supportive nutrition education. ADHS is working with the counties to provide WIC 
services, and this opportunity is to provide additional community support as needed.  
 
1.1. WIC Nutrition Services: 
 
1.1.1. The WIC Program accomplishes this goal by providing participant -centered services (PCS) 
including nutrition and breastfeeding information and support, specific supplemental healthy 
foods through the issuance of food benefits that can be used at ADHS -approved grocery 
stores, and referrals to other health and human services as an adjunct to good health care 
during critical times of growth and development. Service population eligibility for the WIC 
Program is based upon federal regulations and includes participant category (pregnant and 
breastfeeding women, postpartum women, and infants or children under five (5) years of 
age), a household income at or below 185% of federal poverty guidelines, residence within 
the service area, and nutrition risks,  
 
1.1.2. To be considered as a WIC Local Agency, the organization must be a local public or private 
non-profit organization, county health department, or tribal entity under contract with ADHS 
to provide WIC services according to the WIC Program federal regulations and state policies 
and procedures. The State awards a WIC Contract based on the ability of the organization 
to provide WIC services, potentially eligible population, need, response to the Scope of Work, 
information technology capacity, and available funds. The State determines and awards the 
WIC Contract amount based on a funding formula using several factors such as a base level 
for a program to function, the amount of caseload negotiated with each Local Agency, and 
quality of performance, and 
 
1.1.3. Specific objectives for nutrition services (based on Healthy People 2030 Goals) are:  
 
1.1.3.1. To increase the incidence of women initiating breastfeeding; 
 
1.1.31.2. To increase the duration of women breastfeeding for the first  six (6) months of 
their baby’s life; 
 
1.1.3.3. To increase the duration of women breastfeeding for the first one (1) year of their 
baby’s life; 
 
1.1.3.4. To increase the rate infants are exclusively breastfed at three (3) months and at 
six (6) months; 
 
1.1.3.5. To reduce the proportion of adults who are considered obese; 
 
1.1.3.6. To reduce the proportion of children ages two (2) to five (5) who are considered 
obese;

<!-- page 260 -->

Emily Ngo 
 
 
1.1.3.7. To increase the consumption and variety of fruits and vegetables by those age 
two (2) and older; 
 
1.1.3.8. To increase the consumption of whole grains by those age two (2) and older; 
and 
 
1.1.3.9. To reduce household food insecurity and hunger. 
 
1.2. BFPC: 
 
1.2.1. Breastfeeding peer counselors add a critical dimension to WIC’s efforts to help women initiate 
and continue breastfeeding. WIC breastfeeding peer counselors provide a valuable service 
to their communities, addressing barriers to breastfeeding by offering b reastfeeding 
education, support, and role modeling. They are familiar with the resources available to WIC 
participants and the questions a new breastfeeding mother may ask, and recognize when to 
refer mothers to other resources when issues outside their sc ope of practice arise. In 2020 
the USDA Food and Nutrition Service (FNS) released their new national breastfeeding 
campaign titled WIC Breastfeeding Support: Learn Together. Grow Together. As a part of 
this, FNS has developed training and technical assistance to equip WIC Programs with a 
framework for designing and maintaining peer counseling programs. Developing this training 
included formative research to understand barriers and motivators to implementing and 
sustaining peer counseling programs and develo p training curricula. This Peer Counseling 
Training for WIC Managers, which is a training and technical assistance project that will be 
used as a model to aid WIC in designing, building, maintaining, and sustaining peer 
counseling programs that will improve breastfeeding initiation and duration rates. Arizona will 
be using this program to guide and develop the state peer counseling program, 
 
1.2.2. The discretionary peer counseling services are considered an adjunct support to WIC 
breastfeeding services to help ac hieve the WIC Nutrition Services objectives regarding 
breastfeeding, and 
 
1.2.3. Peer counseling has been a significant factor in improving initiation and duration rates of 
breastfeeding among women in a variety of settings, including disadvantaged and WIC 
populations representing diverse cultural backgrounds and geographical locations. Peer 
counselors are especially effective in communities where role models for breastfeeding 
behaviors, knowledgeable health care providers, and cultural practices that include 
breastfeeding as the norm, are scarce. Combining peer counseling with the ongoing WIC 
breastfeeding promotion and support efforts has the potential to significantly impact 
breastfeeding rates among WIC participants, and most significantly, increase the harder to 
achieve breastfeeding duration rates. WIC Local Agencies are strongly encouraged to 
provide peer counseling services in addition to the ongoing breastfeeding support to their 
WIC participants. Contractors must provide a breastfeeding friendly environmen t and have 
policies to accommodate participants and staff who are breastfeeding. 
 
1.3. The subrecipient has the discretion to determine how the award amount is utilized and should provide a 
detailed outline of costs using a Budget Worksheet provided by ADHS The Grantee must adhere to the 
funding restrictions as outlined in the Arizona Policy and Procedure Manual.  
 
1.4. Based on program caseload and service area, funding will be allocated appropriately to all active 
subrecipients each Grant year for a five (5) year grant period. Budgets and work plans will be reviewed 
annually and may be decreased based on:

1.4.1. Changes to USDA funding allocation. 
 
1.4.2. Changes to USDA caps on WIC services. 
 
1.4.3. Failure to meet the goals and activities outlined in this Scope of Services. 
 
1.5. The funds are awarded to ADHS and awarded to subrecipients through this Intergovernmental 
Agreement (IGA) and the Contract Application process. 
 
1.6. ADHS has the right to partially award based on the needs and areas being serviced.  
 
2. Objective 
 
The Subrecipient shall: 
 
2.1 At a minimum, provide WIC services while allowing each Local Agency to provide, at their discretion, 
Breastfeeding Peer Counseling Services. 
 
2.2 In the event the Arizona WIC Program funding is depleted (e.g. government shutdown) the Subrecipient 
may request permission to continue operating their program utilizing local funds. The Subrecipient shall 
contact the Arizona WIC Director to discuss the feasibility of sustaining clinic operations and participant 
food redemption at authorized vendor locations. The Subrecipient may transfer funds to ADHS to ensure 
continued operation. 
 
2.3 Additional monies may be awarded under this contract for WIC Special Projects related to, but not limited 
to general infrastructure, breastfeeding promotion, nutrition services, information system enhancement 
efforts and client retention efforts. 
 
3. Scope of Service 
 
3.1. WIC Services: 
 
The WIC Program Subrecipient shall perform all the work required to administer and provide WIC services 
to eligible participants according to the Arizona WIC Program Policies and Procedures Manual (WIC 
PPM). These include, but are not limited to, the following activities: 
 
3.1.1. Perform WIC certification procedures such as categorical and income screening and health 
and nutrition assessments, 
 
3.1.2. Provide participant centered nutrition and breastfeeding support services to WIC participants, 
 
3.1.3. Provide the mandatory and appropriate additional referrals reflecting the needs of the 
individual WIC participants, 
 
3.1.4. Issue WIC food benefits tailored to meet the needs of the participants, 
 
3.1.5. Ensure the collection and recording of accurate information, 
 
3.1.6. Provide professional training, mentoring and monitoring of WIC staff competencies 
necessary for delivery of required services, 
 
3.1.7. Provide administrative functions for operation of the WIC Program, and

3.1.8. Prepare and submit all required plans/reports in accordance with this contract and the WIC 
PPM. 
 
3.2. BFPC Services: 
 
3.2.1. WIC BFPC Subrecipients shall perform all the work required to administer and provide 
additional breastfeeding support services to WIC mothers following the USDA WIC 
Breastfeeding Curriculum guidelines for peer counseling services and according to the 
Contractor’s proposal in or der to increase the breastfeeding rate and duration of 
breastfeeding, and 
 
3.2.2. Provide supervision, mentoring, monitoring, and evaluation of peer counselors, when 
appropriate. 
 
4. Tasks 
 
4.1. The WIC Program provides nutrition and breastfeeding support and information, supplemental nutritious 
foods, and referrals to other health and social services as an adjunct to good health care during critical 
times of growth and development, in order to prevent the occurrence of health problems and to improve 
the health status of eligible women, infants and children. 
 
4.2. Nutrition education including breastfeeding promotion and support as part of the WIC program, shall be 
designed to achieve the following two broad goals:  
 
4.2.1. Emphasize the relationship between nutrition, physical activity, and health with special 
emphasis on the nutritional needs of pregnant, postpartum, and breastfeeding women, 
infants, and children under five years of age, and raise awareness about the dangers of using 
drugs and other harmful substances during pregnancy and while breastfeeding.  
 
4.2.2. Assist the individual who is at nutritional risk in improving health status and achieving a 
positive change in dietary and physical activity habits, and in the prevention of nutrition-
related problems through optimal use  of supplemental foods and other nutritious foods. This 
is to be taught in the context of the ethnic, cultural, and geographic preferences of the 
participants and with consideration for educational and environmental limitations 
experienced by the participants. 
 
4.3. WIC Services Outreach, Retention, and Referrals: 
 
The Subrecipient shall: 
 
4.3.1. Conduct outreach activities targeting underserved populations by developing written and 
verbal presentations, or utilizing available materials, and/or promoting WIC on social media 
platforms to inform potentially WIC eligible individuals of the availability of the WIC Program 
and to explain the benefits of participation in accordance with the WIC PPM and this Contract, 
 
4.3.2. Conduct retention activities to maintain and increase the participation of current WIC 
enrollees, 
 
4.3.3. Establish community partnerships with, at a minimum, the mandatory referral agencies, local 
hospitals, OB/GYN and pediatricians offices, and provide regular contacts in accordance with 
the WIC PPM and this Contract,

4.3.4. Establish community partnerships with community organizations such as food banks, human 
and social services, school districts, etc. in accordance with the WIC PPM and this Contract,

4.3.5. Establish a referral system with breastfeeding/lactation specialists, including, but not limited

4.4. Additional BFPC Outreach Tasks: 
 
4.4.1. Develop and document an internal referral link between the WIC Program and WIC Peer

Peer Counselors, participation of Peer Counselors in WIC clinic meetings, and/or additional 
training for WIC clinic staff and Peer Counselors. 
 
4.5. Participant Records: 
 
4.5.1. Document in Health and Nutrition Delivery System (HANDS) and maintain documentation of 
participant centered certification and administrative procedures as described in the WIC 
PPM, including, but not limited to, the following: 
 
4.5.1.1. Eligibility and ineligibility determinations; 
 
4.5.1.2. Nutrition assessments; 
 
4.5.1.3. Nutrition and breastfeeding education and support; 
 
4.5.1.4. Nutrition and breastfeeding counseling; 
 
4.5.1.5. Facilitate behavioral goal setting; 
 
4.5.1.6. Appropriate referrals; 
 
4.5.1.7. Program fraud; and 
 
4.5.1.8. Food Benefit Issuances. 
 
4.5.2. Maintain inventory and accountability records, as set forth in the WIC PPM for Electronic 
Benefits Transfer (EBT) WIC (eWIC) cards, 
 
4.5.3. Maintain records in accordance with the PPM, 
 
4.5.4. Assure participant confidentiality by obtaining written permission from affected program 
participant(s) prior to the release of participant information to any agency. The Contractor 
shall have a written agreement, completed in accordance with 7 CFR 246.26(d) , and the 
Arizona WIC Policy and Procedure Manual, with any agency or program that will share 
participant information. The above federal regulation details the implementation of a written 
agreement and state plan to regulate use and disclosure of confidential applicant and 
participant information,

Amendment  
 
ARIZONA DEPARTMENT

150 18th Ave Suite 530

4.5.5. Staff shall only access the WIC Computer Data System and client files for business related 
reasons, 
 
4.5.6. Ensure paper files containing confidential participant information are stored in a secure 
location in the clinic, archived when appropriate, and destroyed according to the retention 
schedule.  All files should be destroyed in an appropriate manner, 
 
4.5.7. The Subrecipient shall notify the State Agency immediately by telephone call and email upon 
the discovery of a breach of a participant’s confidentiality, 
 
4.5.8. The Subrecipient shall immediately investigate such security incidents, breaches, or 
unauthorized use or disclosure of participant information. These investigations shall include: 
 
4.5.8.1. These investigations shall include: 
 
4.5.8.1.1. What data elements were involved and the extent of the data 
involved in the breach; 
 
4.5.8.1.2. A description of the unauthorized person(s) known or 
reasonably believed to have improperly used or disclosed the 
protected information; 
 
4.5.8.1.3. A description of where the protected information is believed to 
have been improperly transmitted, sent, or utilized; 
 
4.5.8.1.4. A description of the probable causes of the improper use or 
disclosure; and 
 
4.5.8.1.5. Whether Arizona Revised Statutes (A.R.S) § 18 -552 or any 
other federal or state laws requiring individual notifications of 
breaches are triggered. 
 
4.5.9. The Subrecipient shall provide a written report of the investigation to the Chief of the 
BNPA/WIC Director and Chief of the Office of Program Integrity within ten (10) working days 
of the discovery of the breach or unauthorized use or disclosure.  The report shall include the 
information specified above, as well as a full, detailed corrective action plan, including 
information on measures that were taken to halt and/or contain the improper use or 
disclosure, and 
 
4.5.10. The Subrecipient shall notify individuals of the breach or unauthorized use or disclosure when 
notification is required under state or federal law and shall pay any costs of such notifications, 
as well as any costs associated with the breach using non-WIC funds. The BNPA Chief/WIC 
Director, Chief of the Office of Program Integrity,  and Assistant Attorney General shall 
approve the time, manner and content of any such notifications. The Local Agency shall 
arrange and pay for any mitigation (e.g. LifeLock) for participants at  risk for identity theft 
because of breach of security of information. 
 
4.6. Service Delivery and Program Rules: 
 
4.6.1. Determine eligibility of persons requesting WIC services by screening individuals in 
accordance with procedures set forth in the WIC PPM.

4.6.1.1. Eligible program participants shall include the following categories whose 
household income does not exceed 185% of the current designated federal 
poverty guidelines, who have a nutritional risk as defined in the WIC PPM, and 
reside in Arizona as defined by the WIC PPM: 
 
4.6.1.1.1. Pregnant women, breastfeeding women up to twelve (12) 
months post-partum, and non- breastfeeding women up to six 
(6) months following the end of a pregnancy; 
 
4.6.1.1.2. Infants from birth to age one (1); and 
 
4.6.1.1.3. Children from age one (1) year up to five (5) years. 
 
4.6.2. Provide complete nutrition assessment and document results and follow-up as outlined in the 
Arizona WIC Policy and Procedure Manual, 
 
4.6.3. Provide participant -centered nutrition education to participants and appropriately utilize 
materials provided by the State, 
 
4.6.4. Facilitate goal setting for behavioral change and follow-up on goals set, 
 
4.6.5. Promote breastfeeding to WIC participants and provide breastfeeding education and support, 
and refer to and promote the Breastfeeding Peer Counseling Program, when appropriate, 
 
4.6.6. Prescribe and tailor a food package appropriate to the participant's nutritional risk(s), the 
amount of formula consumed, and cultural preferences and issue food benefits as set forth 
in the WIC PPM, 
 
4.6.7. Provide program participants with information about available health and social services to 
which the participant could be referred. The participant shall be provided with written 
information regarding community services and referrals in accordance with the WIC PPM 
and Local Agency referral procedures, 
 
4.6.8. Coordinate WIC Services with other health and social services available within the service 
area, including but not limited to immunizations, voter registration, and breastfeeding support, 
 
4.6.9. Provide information, check for understanding, and document instruction to participants on 
program rules, regulations, WIC approved foods, use of eWIC cards, and food benefit use 
and redemption. The program instruction shall be documented in the participant's record as 
outlined in the WIC PPM, and 
 
4.6.10. Consider the impact of scheduling practices, hours of operation, and clinic closures on 
caseload and WIC applicants’ access to services. 
 
4.6.11. Additional BFPC Service Delivery Tasks: 
 
4.6.11.1. Accept referrals generated from calls to the ADHS Pregnancy and Breastfeeding 
Hotline into the Peer Counseling Program, and 
 
4.6.11.2. Provide peer counseling services, when appropriate. Services shall be made 
available outside of usual clinic hours and outside of the WIC clinic, but may also

4.7. Participant Centered Nutrition Education: 
 
4.7.1. Provide and document participant-centered nutrition education to all WIC adult participants, 
and to parents or caretakers of participants according to the guidelines of the State Nutrition 
Services Plans. Nutrition education contacts shall be made availabl e quarterly to all adult 
participants and the Authorized Representatives of infant and child participants certified. 
Nutrition education contacts shall be scheduled on a periodic basis by the local agency, but 
such contacts do not necessarily need to take place in each quarter of the certification period, 
 
4.7.2. Provide and document professional supervision, mentoring and monitoring of staff at the 
clinic level on a regular basis, in accordance with Local Agency Self -Assessment 
requirements and as often as necessary, to ensure competence, 
 
4.7.3. Offer, as often as necessary, high - and medium -risk nutrition education/counseling by a 
Registered Dietitian Nutritionist (RDN) to all participants deemed high-risk upon assessment. 
As defined in the WIC PPM Local Agencies may designate a Bachelor's degree nutritionist 
or Nutrition and Dietetic Technicians, registered (NDTR) to serve as a WIC Nutritionist to 
provide medium-risk counseling to participants under the supervision of the RDN, 
 
4.7.4. Expend a minimum of seven percent (7%) of the total amount the Subrec ipient receives for 
provision of WIC services each contract year on salary, employee related expenses, travel 
expenses, continuing education, and training for one or more Registered Dietitian 
Nutritionists (RDN) providing high risk services. The agency may  pay another Arizona WIC 
Local Agency or RDN contractor for RDN services as approved by the State WIC Director or 
the State WIC Director's designee. If the seven percent (7%) of the total amount the 
Subrecipient receives for provision of WIC services is not expended for employee related 
expenses, travel expenses, continuing education, and training expenses for RDNs, ADHS 
may request the Subrecipient to return an amount equal to the difference between the seven 
percent (7%) of the total amount the Subrecipient receives for provision of WIC services and 
the actual amount expended, 
 
4.7.5. Expend for nutrition education activities an aggregate amount that is not less than the sum 
of one-sixth (1/6) of the amount the Subrecipient receives for provision of WIC services each 
contract year. If the one-sixth (1/6) amount is not expended for nutrition education activities, 
ADHS may request the Subrecipient to return an amount equal to the difference between the 
one-sixth (1/6) requirement and the actual amount expended if ADHS must pay a penalty to 
the Federal Government. 
 
4.7.5.1. Costs that can be applied to meet the one-sixth (1/6) requirement for nutrition 
education include: 
 
4.7.5.1.1. Salary and other costs for time spent on nutrition education, 
whether with an individual or group. 
 
4.7.5.1.2. The cost of procuring and producing nutrition education 
materials. 
 
4.7.5.1.3. The cost of training nutrition educators, including costs related 
to conducting training sessions and purchasing and producing 
training materials;

4.8. Staffing 
 
4.8.1. Designate a WIC Program Director/Manager who is an RDN, with previous WIC and/or 
community health experience to manage and administer the WIC Program and may provide 
high-risk nutrition counseling and/or formula authorization to WIC participants.  If an RDN is 
on staff to provide the WIC RDN services and with prior approval from ADHS, the Contractor 
shall designate a Director with a minimum of an undergraduate degree from an accredited 
institution in nutrition (community nutrition, public health nutrition, nutrition education, human 
nutrition or nutrition science) or a related field such as home economics or biochemistry with 
an emphasis in nutritional science or public health administration.  With prior approval from 
ADHS, number of years working in a WIC program may substitute for some years of 
education,
 
 
4.8.2. Identify an RDN to serve as the Local Agency Nutrition Coordinator as defined in the WIC 
PPM. The Nutrition Coordinator shall oversee all WIC nutrition services for the Local Agency.  
If a Local Agency has barriers to this staffing standard, they must be submitted in writing to 
ADHS with their plan for coordination of nutrition services within the Local Agency to be 
approved by ADHS,
 
 
4.8.3. Provide an appropriate number of RDNs, based on assigned caseload, to perform high -risk 
and medium -risk counseling, formula authorization, and as necessary, certification of 
participants. The Subrecipient shall provide the RDN services in a number proportional to the 
agency's high-risk caseload in accordance with the WIC PPM. The Subrecipient shall hire 
graduates with a minimum of a Master’s or Bachelor’s degree from an accredited institution 
in Nutritional Sciences, Community Nutrition, Clinical Nutrition,  Dietetics, Public Health 
Nutrition, or Home Economics with emphasis in Nutrition or Nutrition and Dietetic 
Technicians, registered to do medium -risk counseling, formula authorization, and as

necessary, participant certification under the direction of an R DN. If a Local Agency has a

4.8.4. Provide an appropriate number of adequately trained certification speci alists, based on

instructing on Food Benefits in the Arizona WIC Program. Staff should be observed and

4.8.5. Provide an appropriate number of adequately trained nutrition education specialists, based 
on caseload, to provide certification and nutrition education services to participants in 
accordance with the WIC PPM. Such individuals shall have the minimum of a high school 
diploma with nutrition experience, education, and training that have been certified by local or 
state agencies to be competent for the position. An Associate or Bachelor's degree is highly 
preferred. These individuals shall meet the ADHS competencies for Nutrition Education 
Specialists as set forth in the WIC PPM prior to certifying applicants for participation and/or 
providing nutrition services in the Arizona WIC Program. Staff shall be observed and certified 
by the supervising authority to be competent in an activity before being allowed to perform 
on their own without supervision,
 
 
4.8.6. Identify an IBCLC to serve as the local agency Breastfeeding Coordinator as defined in the 
WIC PPM. The Breastfeeding Coordinator shall oversee all WIC breastfeeding services for 
the local agency to ensure all participants have access to breastfeeding promotion and 
support services. If a local agency has barriers to this staffing standard, they shall submit in 
writing to ADHS with their plan for coordination of breas tfeeding services within the local 
agency to be approved by ADHS,
 
 
4.8.7. Identify a Training Coordinator as defined in the WIC PPM. The Training Coordinator  shall 
oversee and facilitate both new employee and ongoing WIC training for the Local Agency 
including certifying that staff has met competencies prior to providing services without 
supervision. If a Local Agency has barriers to this staffing standard, they shall submit in 
writing to ADHS their plan for coordination of training services within the Local Agency to be 
approved by ADHS, 
 
4.8.8. Identify an Outreach Coordinator who shall oversee and facilitate activities and efforts to 
retain current WIC enrollees and outr each to potentially eligible populations not currently 
enrolled in WIC, and participate in outreach -related workgroups. If a Local Agency has 
barriers to this staffing standard, they shall submit in writing to ADHS their plan for 
coordination of outreach activities within the Local Agency to be approved by ADHS, 
 
4.8.9. Provide staff to conduct outreach activities targeting high risk and underserved populations, 
including but not limited to homeless and migrants, by developing written and verbal 
presentations or utilizing available materials to inform the potentially eligible individuals of the 
availability of WIC Program and explain the benefits of participation, and

4.9. Additional BFPC Service Staffing:

consultation duties for any reason, and

others to succeed at breastfeeding, and similarities with the WIC population the p rogram

peer counselor qualifications shall be approved by ADHS before hire. When possible, peer 
counselors should be current or previous WIC participants.

8. Performance Standards and Award Program 
 
8.1. Upon Grant finalization, ADHS shall notify the Subrecipient by email of the assigned caseload and, 
throughout the term of the Grant, any changes to the assigned caseload. The Subrecipient shall maintain 
an average monthly participation level in accordance with the following table: 
 
Caseload Assignment    % Maintained 
<10,000 participants/month    97% 
10,000 to <49,999 participants/month   98% 
>50,000 participants/month    99% 
 
8.2. If, after each quarter of the Federal Fiscal Year (October through September), the Subrecipient has not 
attained the required participation level, ADHS will have the option of reducing the assigned caseload

10. Areas of Service 
Underserved communities that have eligible populations at or below 185% of the Federal Poverty Guidelines 
within the State of Arizona.  
 
11. Target Population 
 
WIC services nutritionally at -risk and low -income pregnant, breastfeeding, and non -breastfeeding postpartum 
women, infants, and children up to age five (5). 
 
11.1. Eligible populations may also include:  
 
11.1.1. Migrant and agricultural workers.   
 
11.1.2. Pregnant women, especially women in the early months of pregnancy. 
 
11.1.3. Homeless individuals and facilities.   
 
11.1.4. Infants and children under the care of foster parents and/or the Department of Child Safety 
(DCS).  
 
11.1.5. Persons employed or residing in rural areas. 
 
11.1.6. Working families. 
 
11.1.7. Recipients of Temporary Assistance for Needy Families (TANF).  
 
11.1.8. Recipients of Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program (SNAP).  
 
11.1.9. Participants in the Child and Adult Care Food Program (CACFP). 
 
11.1.10. Women enrolled in substance abuse programs.  
 
11.1.11. Persons enrolled in the Arizona Health Care Cost Containment System (AHCCCS).  
 
11.1.12. Minority and immigrant populations.

Memorandum of Understanding
Between the Navajo County Board of Supervisors and the Navajo County
Sheriff to Support Peer Counseling In Persons Suffering from Substance
Abuse Disorders (“SUDs”)
This Memorandum  of Understanding  (MOU)  is made and entered  into
by and between  the Navajo  County  Board of Supervisors “hereinafter referred
to as “ County ”_ and the Navajo County Sheriff’s Office  (hereinafter referred
to as " NCSO")
Background
WHEREAS, County is a political subdivision of the State of Arizona, and
pursuant to A.R.S. § 11-201, Navajo County may enter into contracts as
necessary for the exercise of its powers; and,
WHEREAS, the Navajo County Sheriff is a duly elected public official
and is responsible for running the Navajo County jail; and
WHEREAS, the Navajo County jail, by virtue of its purpose, encounters a
large number of inmates suffering from substance abuse disorders; and
WHEREAS, County has received settlement funds through the One
Arizona Distribution of Opioid Settlement Funds Agreement (“Settlement”)
which are to be spent on opioid abatement strategies as set out in “Exhibit A” of
the One Arizona Agreement which can be found here:
 
https://static1.squarespace.com/static/6462aca0912c6c433ed7b5cd/t/6462b93123
89006b605d9dbc/1684191537934/One+AZ+Agmt+-
+Ex+A+%28Approved+Purposes%29.pdf; and
WHEREAS, County desires to enter into this Memorandum of
Understanding with the NCSO to fulfill the opioid abatement strategies using
settlement funding;
NOW THEREFORE, the parties enter into this MOU  upon the terms and
conditions set forth below.

the failure of NCSO’s failure to comply with any material obligation, term or
condition in this MOU, or to make satisfactory progress in performing its
obligations under this MOU.
Funding

$106,396.32 in year one, $102,308.00 in year two, and $105,378.00 in year three.

consistent with the opioid abatement strategies set forth in the One Arizona

requirements and the terms and conditions of the Settlement.

provide records and documentation to County during the term of this MOU and
after expiration of termination of this MOU.   NCSO shall comply with requests

1. How does the project support the County’s Guiding Principles? Does the project benefit 
the population most impacted in Navajo County Community? 
 
The Navajo County Sheriff’s Office is committed to providing critical care and services to 
those who have been most affected by opioid epidemic by creating a system of 
programs that care for those who are placed into the Navajo County Adult Detention 
Center.  
The proposed project directly supports Navajo County's commitment to public health 
and safety while serving our most vulnerable populations. Recent data, obtained from 
individuals who have been screened for the COPE program between April 2023 and April 
2025, continues to demonstrate the severe impact that opioid abuse has in Navajo 
County. A total of 6945 individuals were screened for the COPE program - 14% (934) 
responded that they used opiates; 8% (581) responded that they had overdosed at least 
once; of those reporting at least one overdose, 15% (87) reported 5 or more overdose 
incidents.  
 
This proposal aims to connect peer support staff with incarcerated individuals identified 
as having SUD or co-occurring disorders (SUD in combination with a mental health 
diagnosis) who are in general population and who are not already engaged in NCSO’s in-
custody residential treatment program (RSAT). The program will allow us to provide 
recovery orientated services in custody with limited resources utilizing peer support 
staff.  
 
2. Does this project have a direct link to addressing the opioid crisis? Is it a response to the 
impacts of the epidemic? 
This project establishes a clear connection to addressing the opioid crisis through: 
• Providing direct support during incarceration, a critical intervention point 
• Creating a bridge to community-based treatment and recovery services 
• Reducing the risk of overdose upon release, a period of particularly high 
vulnerability 
 
Peer support staff embedded within the Navajo County Jail will serve as a vital bridge 
between inmates and the Sheriff's Office's commitment to providing critical care and 
services to those who have been most affected by opioid epidemic. These specially 
trained staff members embody innovative techniques by offering lived experience and 
understanding that traditional correctional approaches may miss the mark. Their unique 
position allows them to connect with inmates on a deeper level, helping to defuse 
tensions, identify substance use disorder and co-occurring condition concerns early, and 
create pathways for rehabilitation. This person-centered approach demonstrates 
sensitivity to individual needs while supporting the jail's security protocols. By fostering

<!-- page 294 -->
an environment of trust and understanding, peer support staff strengthen the Sheriff's 
Office's ability to maintain safety through prevention and intervention rather than just 
reaction. Their presence reflects NCSO's dedication to using progressive resources that 
benefit both the inmate population and the broader community. 
 
Supportive services will include;  
• Identifying needs and goals 
• Increasing practical knowledge of addiction 
• One on one support 
• Reentry/Recovery planning 
• May facilitate group sessions 
 
FTE #1 Schedule: 
Sunday: 8:00 AM - 4:30 PM (8 hours) 
Monday: 8:00 AM - 4:30 PM (8 hours) 
Tuesday: 8:00 AM - 4:30 PM (8 hours) 
Wednesday: 8:00 AM - 4:30 PM (8 hours) 
Saturday: 8:00 AM - 4:30 PM (8 hours)

Friday: 11:00 AM – 8:00 PM (8 hours) 
Saturday: 11:00 AM – 8:00 PM (8 hours) 
Sunday: 11:00AM – 8:00 PM (8 hours) 
Monday & Tuesday: OFF 
 
Release and re-entry are the last component of door to door program services. Back 
door (release) working with our local addiction stakeholders to ensure our program was 
congruent to programs offered by their services, we would be able to transition 
offenders from an in-custody treatment plan to either a residential or outpatient service 
once released. This would allow for our Peer mentors and re-entry coordinators to do a 
warm handoff increasing the viability of successful reintroduction to community from 
custody.  
 
3. Which allowable expense might the project fit into? 
 
 Increase use of Medication Assisted Treatment 
o Provide MAT and MOUD to arrestees that meet program requirements.

<!-- page 295 -->
 Improve treatment in jails.  
o Implement a comprehensive peer support program service that works to 
connect incarcerated individuals with peers who can provide in-custody 
support as well as assist in warm-hand off to community-based services. 
 Fund warm hand-off programs and recovery services. 
o By utilizing peer support staff to assist in identifying needs and goals that 
can then be the foundation for reentry planning that includes warm hand 
off to community resources 
o By utilizing peer support staff to begin working with incarcerated 
individuals to provide support and a model for successful recovery  
 Enrich prevention strategies. 
o Peer support staff will work with NCSO RSAT and Reentry Services as well 
as local community stakeholders to develop processes that allow for 
seamless and efficient connection to community resources immediately 
upon release and beyond as inmates integrate back into the community.  
 Expand harm reduction programs. 
o Peer supports will work with individuals to identify the best recovery 
plan, including use of harm reduction strategies. 
 Support data collection and research. 
o Utilizing peer mentors and coordinators, track available data collection 
and share results utilizing the state-wide database project that we are a 
part of using Opioid Settlement funds awarded to the 5 counties that 
currently have reentry programs). We will also create MOU’s to allow 
data sharing with community stake holders to provide real time data that 
serves to drive new and innovative program initiatives.

How could the project be sustained after the initial opioid settlement agreement 
funding investment?

Attorney General’s Office to sustain the cost for the program long term.

budget to offset county funding liability. 
 
 
6. Is the project evidence based or evidence informed? Please detail the evidence 
based/informed practices the project will utilize to establish an effective process, as well 
as detailed project evaluation to measure effectiveness. 
 
Research demonstrates that peer support programs in correctional settings are a vital 
evidence-based practice for addressing opioid use disorder (OUD) and that they 
demonstrate effectiveness in promoting recovery and reducing recidivism. Studies 
published in the Journal of Addiction Medicine (Hamilton et al., 2022) highlight how 
peer support specialists, drawing from their lived experience with substance use, 
recovery, and justice involvement are uniquely positioned to build trust and 
engagement with incarcerated individuals with OUD. This trust-based relationship 
proves especially powerful in jail settings, where individuals may be resistant to 
traditional treatment approaches. Research by Martin et al. (2021) in the International 
Journal of Prisoner Health indicates that when peer support is integrated into 
correctional healthcare services, there are measurable improvements in treatment

<!-- page 298 -->
adherence, particularly with MAT/MOUD services (Medication Assisted 
Treatment/Medications for Opioid Use Disorder). 
 
The evidence supporting peer support programs goes beyond the duration an individual 
is incarcerated, with studies showing their crucial role in successful community 
reintegration. According to a systematic review by Gormley et al. (2021), individuals 
who engage with peer support specialists while incarcerated are more likely to continue 
their recovery journey post-release, with higher rates of treatment engagement and 
lower rates of relapse. This effectiveness stems from peers' ability to navigate both the 
correctional environment and recovery landscape, providing practical guidance while 
offering hope through their own recovery stories. Stack et al. (2022) particularly 
emphasizes how peer support programs create a bridge between jail-based treatment 
and community services, helping to close the critical gap where many individuals 
traditionally fall through the cracks of care. These evidence-based outcomes have led 
correctional facilities nationwide to increasingly recognize peer support as an essential 
component of comprehensive OUD treatment strategies. 
 
By using peer mentors, we bring real life specialists who have their own story of 
recovery and utilize their own experience to help those by promoting recovery-oriented 
environments. Investing in peer support specialists is a valuable addition to correctional 
facilities, providing both immediate support for incarcerated individuals and potential 
long-term benefits for successful reintegration into society. 
 
Project evaluation will use the following quantitative metrics: 
Number of inmates who requested PSS services 
Frequency of PSS encounters with inmates 
Number of referrals to community-based treatment from PSS 
Recidivism rates of inmates who engage with PSS 
Project Evaluation will use the following qualitative metrics: 
Participant entry interviews vs. Participant exit interviews 
Participant exit evaluation surveys 
Correctional staff feedback surveys (including medical staff) – quarterly  
 
7. How long would it take to complete the project? 
 
The program implemented would not have a completion date. The program is designed 
to be on going, based on funding availability.

<!-- page 299 -->
References 
 
Hamilton, J., Ti, L., Korchinski, M., et al. (2022). Peer support specialists: an underutilized 
resource in the Criminal Justice System for Opioid Use Disorder Management. Journal of 
Addiction Medicine, 16(2), 235-238. 
 
Martin, M., Phillips, M.A., Saxon, M., Love, K. (2021). Recovery support specialists inside 
the jail: A program description of treatment engagement for opioid use disorder. 
International Journal of Prisoner Health. 
 
Gormley, M.A., Pericot-Valverde, I., Diaz, L. (2021). Effectiveness of peer recovery 
support services on stages of the opioid use disorder treatment cascade: a systematic 
review. Drug and Alcohol Dependence. 
 
Stack, E., Hildebran, C., Leichtling, G. (2022). Peer recovery support services across the 
continuum: In community, hospital, corrections, and treatment and recovery agency 
settings–A narrative review. Journal of Addiction Medicine, 16(2), 156-163

Memorandum of Understanding
Between the Navajo County Board of Supervisors and the Navajo County
Sheriff for Women’s 90-Day Substance Abuse Treatment Program
This Memorandum  of Understanding  (MOU)  is made and entered  into
by and between  the Navajo  County  Board of Supervisors “hereinafter referred
to as “ County ”_ and the Navajo County Sheriff’s Office  (hereinafter referred
to as " NCSO")
Background
WHEREAS, County is a political subdivision of the State of Arizona, and
pursuant to A.R.S. § 11-201, Navajo County may enter into contracts as
necessary for the exercise of its powers; and,
WHEREAS, the Navajo County Sheriff is a duly elected public official
and is responsible for running the Navajo County jail; and
WHEREAS, the Navajo County jail, by virtue of its purpose, encounters a
large number of inmates suffering from substance abuse disorders; and
WHEREAS, County has received settlement funds through the One
Arizona Distribution of Opioid Settlement Funds Agreement (“Settlement”)
which are to be spent on opioid abatement strategies as set out in “Exhibit A” of
the One Arizona Agreement which can be found here:
 
https://static1.squarespace.com/static/6462aca0912c6c433ed7b5cd/t/6462b93123
89006b605d9dbc/1684191537934/One+AZ+Agmt+-
+Ex+A+%28Approved+Purposes%29.pdf; and
WHEREAS, County desires to enter into this Memorandum of
Understanding with the NCSO to fulfill the opioid abatement strategies using
settlement funding;
NOW THEREFORE, the parties enter into this MOU  upon the terms and
conditions set forth below.

<!-- page 307 -->
Proposed Opioid Settlement Agreement Funding Project 
Navajo County Sheriff’s Office Adult Detention Center 
 In Custody Women’s 90 Day Substance Use Treatment Program 
 
1. How does the project support the County’s Guiding Principles? Does the project benefit 
the population most impacted in Navajo County Community? 
 
The Navajo County Sheriff’s Office is committed to providing critical care and services to 
those who have been most affected by opioid epidemic by creating a system of 
programs that care for those who are placed into the Navajo County Adult Detention 
Center.  
 
This project is rooted in Navajo County’s guiding principles of health equity, public 
safety, and evidence-based care. Women incarcerated in Holbrook are a particularly 
vulnerable group—many face cycles of substance use, trauma, and lack of access to 
treatment. On any given day, approximately 50 women are incarcerated in the Navajo 
County Jail, with hundreds cycling through annually.  
NCSO Adult Detention Center currently operates a 90 day, in custody substance use 
treatment program for men, and have significant interest and requests from our female 
inmates about a sister program. 
 
2. Does this project have a direct link to addressing the opioid crisis? Is it a response to the 
impacts of the epidemic? 
 
Yes. Opioid and other substance use are driving factors behind women’s incarceration in 
Navajo County. Substance use disorder was identified as the #1 community health 
concern in the 2023 Community Health Assessment. The county also has elevated rates 
of non-fatal opioid overdoses (47 per 100,000 in 2022), with women of childbearing age 
experiencing some of the highest rates Navajo County Overdose Fatality Review. 
 
3. Which allowable expense might the project fit into? 
 
▪ Increase use of Medication Assisted Treatment 
o The program will provide FDA-approved MAT—including buprenorphine 
and naltrexone—to women diagnosed with opioid use disorder (OUD) 
while they are in custody. MAT is recognized as the gold standard for

<!-- page 308 -->
treating OUD, reducing cravings, the risk of overdose, and relapse. By 
offering MAT in the jail setting, the program ensures continuity of care 
for a high-risk population that often faces significant barriers to accessing 
evidence-based treatment in the community 
▪ Improve treatment in jails.  
o Historically, substance use treatment within jails has been limited or non-
existent, especially for women. This program will implement a full 
continuum of care, including clinical evaluation, individualized treatment 
planning, trauma-informed therapy, and access to MAT. The approach is 
gender-responsive, addressing the unique needs of incarcerated women 
who experience higher rates of trauma, co-occurring mental health 
disorders, and family disruption. This investment will bring Navajo 
County’s jail treatment standards in line with national best practices 
▪ Fund warm hand-off programs and recovery services. 
o The transition from jail back to the community is a critical period, 
especially for women with OUD/SUD, who are at heightened risk for 
relapse and overdose immediately after release. The program will work 
with individuals who are preparing for release back to the community to 
develop individualized reentry plans and coordinate warm hand-offs to 
community-based behavioral health providers and social service agencies 
to promote ongoing support. This connection to recovery services in the 
community increases the odds of sustained recovery and reduces 
recidivism. 
▪ Enrich prevention strategies. 
o Beyond direct treatment, the program will provide evidence-based 
education on substance use, relapse prevention, and harm reduction. 
Participants will learn about overdose risks, safe practices, and how to 
access community resources—including naloxone distribution, support 
groups, and counseling. By equipping women with knowledge and skills 
before release, the program strengthens their ability to make healthy 
choices and avoid future involvement with the justice system. 
▪ Expand harm reduction programs. 
o Reentry staff and Wexford medical staff will work with individuals to 
identify the best recovery plan, including use of harm reduction 
strategies and overdose prevention education. 
▪ Support data collection and research. 
o In partnership with Wexford Medical, collect data and share results 
utilizing the state-wide database project that we are a part of using 
Opioid Settlement funds awarded to the 5 counties that currently have 
reentry programs.

<!-- page 309 -->
4. How much is the project expected to cost? How much is being requested from Opioid 
Settlement Agreement funds for the project? Are there any other entities, organizations 
or grants that could help fund the project? 
 
Estimated annual cost is $240,000, which covers clinical staff, evidence-based SUD 
treatment curriculum, and reentry services including funding for housing support upon 
release from Navajo County Adult Detention Center. The full amount is being requested 
from Opioid Settlement Agreement funds for years 1 and 2. The Sheriff’s Office through 
the Arizona Sheriff’s Association is seeking legislative support to make funding of these 
programs permanent allocations from the state budget to offset county funding liability. 
 
 
ANNUAL BUDGET BREAKDOWN 
Year 1 
Staff & Management Fees for Contracted Medical Provider: $209,000 
Post Release Housing Support Funds: $25,000 
 
Year 2 
Staff & Management Fees for Contracted Medical Provider  
Post Release Housing Support Funds: $25,000 
 
TOTAL 2 YEAR COST: $468,000 
 
 
5. Are there other entities, organizations or grants that could help fund the ongoing costs? 
How could the project be sustained after the initial opioid settlement agreement 
funding investment? 
 
The Sheriff’s Office through the Arizona Sheriff’s Association is seeking legislative 
support to make funding of these programs permanent allocations from the state 
budget to offset county funding liability. 
 
 
6. Is the project evidence based or evidence informed? Please detail the evidence 
based/informed practices the project will utilize to establish an effective process, as well 
as detailed project evaluation to measure effectiveness.

<!-- page 310 -->
This project uses evidence-based and evidence-informed approaches proven effective 
for treating women with substance use disorders (SUD), especially opioid use disorder 
(OUD), in correctional settings. 
 
The program will primarily use the SMART Recovery curriculum for group sessions. A 
2018 longitudinal study found that SMART Recovery is a viable alternative to 12-step 
groups, with comparable outcomes in abstinence and self-efficacy, and may be 
particularly accessible for those who do not connect with traditional abstinence-only 
groups (Zemore et al., 2018). Recent protocols for longitudinal studies continue to 
reinforce the program’s real-world effectiveness across diverse populations (Kelly et al., 
2023), particularly for justice-involved women who may not connect with abstinence-
only approaches. SMART Recovery’s emphasis on self-management, coping skills, and 
relapse prevention aligns with best practices for incarcerated women, many of whom 
have complex trauma histories or co-occurring disorders. 
 
All participants will be screened using the Texas Christian University Drug Screen V 
(TCUDS V) and its Opioid Supplement. The TCUDS V is a validated screening tool widely 
used in criminal justice settings, with strong reliability and validity for identifying SUD 
and opioid-specific risk among justice-involved populations (Knight et al., 2018). Its use 
is recommended in correctional settings for ensuring treatment is provided to those 
most in need (Knight, Simpson & Hiller, 2002). These tools are recommended by the 
Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration (SAMHSA) and the National 
Institute on Drug Abuse (NIDA) for correctional settings. Routine, validated screening 
ensures no one falls through the cracks and allows for tailored treatment planning from 
the outset. 
 
The program will support participants who are taking MAT/MOUD medications in 
custody, including buprenorphine, methadone or naltrexone Extensive research has 
shown that MAT reduces opioid cravings, overdose risk, and recidivism, and is 
particularly effective when paired with behavioral therapy and peer support. National 
guidelines from SAMHSA (SAMHSA, 2020), the American Society of Addiction Medicine 
(ASAM) (ASAM, 2025), and the National Commission on Correctional Health Care 
(NCCHC) (NCCHC 2025 MAT Guidelines) all recommend MAT as the standard of care for 
OUD in correctional environments. 
 
Participants will have access to peer support specialists through our community partner 
HOPE, Inc. Peer Support is a practice backed by strong evidence for improving 
engagement, reducing relapse, and supporting successful reentry. Peer-led support is 
especially powerful for women who may feel isolated or stigmatized by their substance 
use and justice involvement.

<!-- page 311 -->
The program will track and evaluate: 
• Number of women screened with TCUDS V and Opioid Supplement 
• Number identified as needing SUD/OUD treatment 
• MAT participation  
• Participation and completion rates in the 90 day in custody treatment program. 
• Linkage to community-based treatment post-release 
• Recidivism rates of participants  
 
Participant feedback will be collected regularly to inform program adjustments. Data 
will be shared with county stakeholders and used to drive ongoing quality improvement. 
 
This program combines validated screening tools (TCUDS V and Opioid Supplement), 
evidence-based curriculum (SMART Recovery), national best practices (MAT for OUD), 
and ongoing programmatic evaluation. This integrated, evidence-based approach 
maximizes the odds of recovery for women in the Navajo County Jail and aligns with the 
best available evidence on what works to support justice-involved women who suffer 
from a substance use disorder. 
 
7. How long would it take to complete the project? 
 
The program implemented would not have a completion date. The program is designed 
to be on going, based on funding availability.

<!-- page 312 -->
References 
• American Society of Addiction Medicine. (2025). Public Policy Statement on Treatment 
of Opioid Use Disorder in Correctional Settings. 2025-final-pps-on-treatment-of-oud-in-
correctional-settings.pdf 
• Johnstone, S., Dela Cruz, G. A., Kalb, N., Tyagi, S. V., Potenza, M. N., George, T. P., & 
Castle, D. J. (2022). A systematic review of gender-responsive and integrated substance 
use disorder treatment programs for women with co-occurring disorders. The American 
Journal of Drug and Alcohol Abuse, 49(1), 21–42. 
https://doi.org/10.1080/00952990.2022.2130348 
• Kelly JF, Levy SA, Hoeppner BB (2023). An investigation of SMART Recovery: protocol for 
a longitudinal cohort study of individuals making a new recovery attempt from alcohol 
use disorder. BMJ Open, 13:e066898.  doi:10.1136/bmjopen-2022-066898 
 
• Knight, D. K., Blue, T. R., Flynn, P. M., & Knight, K. (2018). The TCU drug screen 5: 
Identifying justice-involved individuals with substance use disorders. Journal of Offender 
Rehabilitation, 57(8), 525–537. https://doi.org/10.1080/10509674.2018.1549180. 
• National Commission on Correctional Health Care. (2025). Jail Guidelines for the Medical 
Treatment of Substance Use Disorders. 2025-MAT-Guidelines-for-Substance-Use-
Disorders-3-6-25.pdf 
• Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration. Medication-Assisted 
Treatment Insider Correctional Facilities. (HHS Publication No. PEP19-MAT-
CORRECTIONS)  Medication-assisted Treatment Inside Correctional Facilities: Addressing 
Medication Diversion 
• Zemore, Sarah E.; Lui, Camilla; Mericle, Amy; Hemberg, Jordana; Kaskutas, Lee Ann. 
(2018). A longitudinal study of the comparative efficacy of Women for Sobriety, LifeRing, 
SMART Recovery, and 12-step groups for those with AUD. Journal of Substance Abuse 
Treatment, 88, 18-26, https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jsat.2018.02.004.

Navajo County - For Official Use Only 
 
August 25, 2025 
Dear Members of the Opioid Steering Committee, 
On behalf of the Navajo County Public Health Services District, I respectfully request approval to 
revise our original WE CAN Save Lives proposal. As community needs evolve and new resources 
emerge, we believe this revision will strengthen the impact of our efforts while preventing 
duplication of services being developed by other agencies, such as AHCCCS, which is creating a 
data collection system and platform. In addition, shifts in federal funding priorities have provided 
new opportunities to better align resources and meet the needs of our community. 
Since the initial proposal, we have made significant progress toward our goals. Most notably, we 
convened a multi-sector Substance Use Disorder (SUD) workgroup to assess service gaps across the 
region. Through this collaborative process, two critical barriers to care were identified: the lack of 
peer support services and the persistent stigma surrounding substance use. Addressing these 
challenges has become a top priority for our stakeholders. 
The insights and feedback gathered through this process directly shaped the strategies in our revised 
proposal. We are confident these adjustments will lead to more targeted, effective, and sustainable 
outcomes for the communities we serve. 
Enclosed for your review is the Navajo County Peer Project, including our request for additional 
funding. 
Thank you for your continued support and commitment to improving health outcomes across 
Arizona. 
Sincerely, 
Amy Stradling 
Outreach & Education Division Manager 
Navajo County Public Health Services District 
600 North 9th Place 
Show Low, AZ 85901 
Amy.Stradling@navajocountyaz.gov

<!-- page 322 -->
Navajo County - For Official Use Only 
Navajo County Peer & Stigma Reduction 
Initiative 
Proposed Opioid Settlement Agreement Funding Project 
Executed: December 2024 (Revised: August 2025) 
Organization: Navajo County Public Health Services District (NCPHSD) 
Project Contacts: 
• Amy Stradling | (928) 532-6050 | amy.stradling@navajocountyaz.gov 
• Alyssa Lemmon | (928) 532-6050 | alyssa.lemmon@navajocountyaz.gov 
 
Project Overview 
The Navajo County Public Health Services District respectfully requests approval to revise our 
original WE CAN Save Lives proposal to support the Navajo County Peer & Stigma Reduction 
Initiative. 
Based on the findings of the Substance Use Disorder (SUD) Workgroup, stakeholders identified 
a critical gap in peer navigation and peer support services. 
This revised proposal will: 
• Expand existing opioid response efforts. 
• Strengthen the SUD Workgroup through targeted peer interventions. 
• Address stigma and service gaps without duplicating services already funded through 
state or partner agencies. 
Progress has already been made toward the original proposal’s goals, including the establishment 
of the SUD Workgroup, implementation of data-sharing agreements, and development of partner 
forms to support data collection. 
All proposed activities align with allowable opioid settlement funding categories and will 
supplement, not supplant, existing State Opioid Response (SOR) resources. 
 
Stakeholders and Collaborative Partners 
The SUD Workgroup includes:

<!-- page 323 -->
Navajo County - For Official Use Only 
• Change Point 
• Community Bridges 
• Summit Healthcare 
• Hope Inc. 
• Hopi Behavioral Health 
• The Re:Center 
• Community Medical Services 
• Navajo County Detention Center 
• NCPHSD Staff 
• Overdose Fatality Review Members 
Together, these partners ensure all strategies are evidence-based, culturally relevant, and 
community-driven. 
 
Identified Service Gaps 
1. Lack of peer support during transitions from hospitals, detention facilities, or treatment 
programs to community-based care. 
2. Persistent stigma around the use of MOUD/MAT and naloxone. 
3. Limited availability of MOUD/MAT providers, particularly in rural and tribal areas. 
 
Project Goals 
• Establish a Peer Navigator/Case Management program within NCPHSD’s Outreach & 
Education Division. 
• Contract with individuals with lived experience to provide warm hand-offs, support 
transitions, and identify barriers to care. 
• Develop and disseminate stigma-reduction messaging that emphasizes recovery-
oriented, person-first language. 
 
Proposed Activities & Allowable Expense Categories 
1. Contract Peer Navigation/Case Management 
• Allowable Category: �� Fund Warm Hand-Off Programs & Recovery Services 
• Contract peer navigators to provide case management, improve referral systems, and 
support individuals across care transitions. 
2. Contract Community-Based Peer Support

<!-- page 324 -->
Navajo County - For Official Use Only 
• Allowable Categories: �� Improve Treatment in Jails & Prisons; �� Provide 
Pregnancy/Postpartum Treatment & Support 
• Collaborate with the Navajo County Detention Center, WIC courts, and probation 
programs to provide peer support during high-risk transitions. 
• Connect individuals to community services at critical points of reentry or postpartum 
vulnerability. 
3. Develop & Disseminate Educational Materials 
• Allowable Categories: �� Broaden Access to Naloxone; �� Expand Harm Reduction 
Programs 
• Engage individuals with lived experience to identify barriers to naloxone access and 
guide messaging. 
• Develop culturally informed, stigma-reduction campaigns using person-first language. 
• Disseminate educational materials through media outlets, partner agencies, and detention 
center reentry programs. 
 
Alignment with Guiding Principles 
• Spend Money to Save Lives – expand naloxone access, fund warm hand-offs, and 
embed peer support. 
• Use Evidence to Guide Spending – employ proven peer recovery models and evidence-
based naloxone distribution strategies. 
• Focus on Racial Equity – integrate tribal representation, adapt messaging to cultural 
contexts, and reach populations disproportionately affected by opioid misuse. 
• Ensure Transparency – collect demographic data, engage the SUD Workgroup in 
decision-making, and incorporate community feedback throughout implementation. 
 
Budget Request 
Total Requested: $300,000 over 3 years ($100,000 annually) 
Planned Uses (all allowable): 
• Contracted peer navigator/case management services. 
• Contracted peer support agency for embedded services in healthcare and justice settings. 
• Educational and stigma-reduction materials, including naloxone awareness campaigns. 
• Program supplies and transportation support not covered by other funding streams.

<!-- page 325 -->
Navajo County - For Official Use Only 
Sustainability Plan 
• Leverage State Opioid Response (SOR) funds to sustain case management services. 
• Strengthen referral systems and cross-agency infrastructure to ensure long-term 
impact. 
• Pursue additional grant funding and partner contributions through the SUD Workgroup. 
 
Evidence Base 
• Peer support interventions increase treatment retention, improve recovery outcomes, and 
reduce relapse. 
• Stigma reduction efforts, including public education and person-first language, increase 
willingness to seek treatment and naloxone use. 
 Project Timeline 
Year Milestones 
Year 
1 
Formalize SUD Workgroup, contract peer services, launch baseline data collection, and 
implement initial peer program. 
Year 
2 
Expand partnerships, increase the number of individuals served, and disseminate stigma-
reduction materials. 
Year 
3 Evaluate project impact, finalize sustainability plan, and expand peer support programs.

Navajo County - For Official Use Only
Intergovernmental Agreement
for
Animal Impoundment Services
between
Navajo County and the City of Holbrook
This Intergovernmental Agreement is made as of December 1st, 2025, (the “Effective Date") by and
between the CITY OF HOLBROOK an Arizona municipal corporation, (“CITY"), and NAVAJO COUNTY
PUBLIC HEALTH, a political subdivision of the State of Arizona ("COUNTY"):
I. RECITALS
1. The parties are authorized by A.R.S. § 11-952 to enter into an agreement to
perform animal control activities.
2. The CITY owns and operates an Animal Control Center at 105 Airport Road in
Holbrook for its own animal control activities.
3. The CITY is willing to temporarily hold animals from the COUNTY at its
Animal Control Center upon the terms and conditions set forth herein.
II. AGREEMENT
1. The Recitals above are incorporated into this Agreement as set forth in full.
2. The COUNTY constructed five (5) exterior dog kennels for the COUNTY to use. The
CITY agrees to give COUNTY access to these five (5) kennels for impoundment of
animals seized throughout the COUNTY. After the 72-hour hold time, the dog
becomes the property of the CITY to do with as it sees fit.
3. The COUNTY agrees to pay CITY a flat fee of $3000 per month for the CITY's
acceptance of up to 30 dogs brought in only by the COUNTY Animal Care &
Control officer. This fee shall be inclusive of all services typically provided by the
CITY in connection with dogs impounded at the Animal Control Center, including
feeding, and if necessary, euthanasia. This fee may be amended from time to time
by mutual agreement of the CITY and the COUNTY. A ny such amendments shall
be in writing and approved by the COUNTY’s Board of Supervisors and
attached hereto.
A. The COUNTY understands that the flat monthly fee does not include medical
treatment for which the services of a veterinarian are required. If such services
are required, the COUNTY will be notified before these services are performed
and may decide against such services, unless the veterinarian determines
emergency work is necessary. If services are agreed, the COUNTY will pay the
billed charges.
B. The COUNTY will pay $100 for any dog or cat brought in by a COUNTY Animal
Care & Control officer to the CITY for rabies observation quarantine unless the
dog owner pays all applicable quarantine fees.
C. The COUNTY agrees to allow the CITY to keep all impoundment fees and
daily maintenance fees collected from dog owners on behalf of the COUNTY.
D. If a dog must be impounded for longer than the statutory 72 - 120-hour
impoundment period due to circumstances beyond the reasonable control of
the COUNTY or CITY, such as a court order or the need to continue to
impound the dog for evidentiary purposes, the parties shall cooperate in good
faith to determine the appropriate additional compensation to CITY.

<!-- page 333 -->
2
Navajo County - For Official Use Only
E. The County agrees to dispose of the bodies of any dogs or cats euthanized
by the City on behalf of the County. The County further agrees to assume all
liability for the City providing euthanasia services on its behalf. The County
agrees that such animals are its responsibility, are not owned by the City, and
will not be admitted into the shelter system. The City’s only role in such cases
is to provide humane euthanasia services. This provision applies to any dogs
or cats brought to the City solely for the purpose of euthanasia.
3. The CITY shall bill the COUNTY monthly. The COUNTY shall make payment within
15 days after receipt of the CITY'S invoice unless a charge is disputed, in which
case the bill will not be paid until both parties cooperate to promptly resolve the
disputed charge.
A. Invoices shall be submitted to:
Navajo  County  Animal  Care & Control
600 N 9 th Pl
Show  Low, AZ 85901
B. Payments shall be mailed to:
City of Holbrook
Attn: Finance Department
P.O. Box 970
Holbrook,  AZ 86025
4. The CITY shall exercise the same degree of care with respect to dogs impounded
by the COUNTY as it exercises with respect to dogs impounded by its own
animal control officers.
5. This IGA shall remain in effect until terminated by mutual agreement of the
parties or by either party giving 30 days written notice of termination  to
the other at the address specified in paragraph 3 . This agreement is
also terminable in accordance with the provisions of A.R.S. § 38-511,
and that statute  is hereby  incorporated  into this agreement  by
reference.
6. In the event the CITY’s Animal Control Center is temporarily unavailable for
use due to maintenance, animal illness/outbreak, or any other condition that
renders the facility unusable for impoundment, the monthly fee charged to
the COUNTY shall be pro-rated for the period during which services are
unavailable.
7. In the event that CITY staff are unavailable to perform essential duties at the
Animal Control Center including but not limited to feeding, watering, cleaning,
administering medications, or conducting euthanasia the COUNTY may, with
TOWN's consent, step in to provide temporary support. Any such assistance
provided by COUNTY staff shall be done without liability to the COUNTY, its
employees, or agents.
IN WITNESS WHEREOF, the authorized representatives of the parties have executed this
IGA as of the Effective Date.

Board of Supervisors Regular 4. b. 11.        
Meeting Date: 11/18/2025  
Title: Contract Renewal between NCPHSD and Health Space
Submitted For: Janelle Linn, Health Director 
Submitted By: Kathryn Mathewson, Environmental Health Program Manager
Department: NC Public Health Services District Division: Code Enforcement Division
Motion before the Board:
Contract renewal between Navajo County Public Health Services District and Health Space
Background:
In 2019, Navajo County Public Health Services District entered a contract with Health Space to provide a
platform for health inspections and the issuance of permits for Environmental Health. Health Space has
sent us an updated contract for a 5-year term, that increases 6% after the second year. In addition, they
now offer new services, including allowing owners to log into their account and pay their invoices, obtain
their health permit, and see their inspections. An additional service would include a portal for new
establishments to submit their applications online. These two new services will greatly reduce the time for
staff and will be more efficient for the County. Included in the contract is the one-time fee to build the
system.

Title: IGA Court Collection and Criminal Justice Services
Submitted For: Bryan Layton, County Manager 
Submitted By: Chelle Ewald, Assistant to the County Manager
Department: Administrative Services
Presented By: Chelle Ewald
Motion before the Board:
Consideration of an Intergovernmental Agreement between Navajo County and the incorporated
municipalities of Holbrook, Pinetop-Lakeside, Show Low, Snowflake, Taylor, and Winslow to improve
efficiency in the operation of the Magistrate Courts.
Background:
The agreement is intended to: 
Have County Court Collection staff perform collection services for the Magistrate Courts.
Continue the practice of having municipal police officers and other law enforcement officers file
criminal misdemeanor cases (other than municipal code violations) in the appropriate Justice Court
for prosecution by the County Attorney and defense by the Public Defender; and
Provide for compensation from each municipality to the County to help defray the costs associated
with these criminal justice services.
This is a one-year renewal of an existing IGA that expired in 2019. 
Recommendation: 
Approve
